\begin{matrix}\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}}&=&\delta_{X_{t}}^{\otimes j_{t}}\otimes t_{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{2}\otimes\delta_{X_{1}}^{\otimes j_{1}}\otimes t_{1}\otimes\delta_{X_{0}}^{\otimes j_{0}},\hfill\\
\widehat{\tau}_{3}^{{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}&=&\delta_{X_{n}}^{\otimes j_{n}}\otimes t_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{t+s+2}\otimes\delta_{X_{t+s+1}}^{\otimes j_{t+s+1}}\otimes t_{t+s+1}\otimes\delta_{X_{t+s}}^{\otimes j_{t+s}}.\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Then, we obtain

S_{r,s,t}^{tw}=\sum_{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1},\hbox{\,}{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}(-1)^{|\tau_{3}^{r}|}b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r+t+1}(\widehat{\tau}_{3}^{{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}\otimes b_{s}^{tw}(\tau_{2}^{s})\otimes\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}}),

where
|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|
and
|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|
denote the sum of their components respectively. Now, make

\begin{matrix}{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}&=&(j^{\prime}_{t+s},j_{t+s-1},\ldots,j_{t+1},j^{\prime}_{t}),\hfill\\
\widehat{\tau}_{2}^{{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}}&=&\delta_{X_{t+s}}^{\otimes j^{\prime}_{t+s}}\otimes t_{t+s}\otimes\delta_{X_{t+s-1}}^{\otimes j_{t+s-1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{t+2}\otimes\delta_{X_{t+1}}^{\otimes j_{t+1}}\otimes t_{t+1}\otimes\delta_{X_{t}}^{\otimes j^{\prime}_{t}}.\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Then, we have

S_{r,s,t}^{tw}=\sum_{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1},\hbox{\,}{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}(-1)^{|\tau_{3}^{r}|}\sum_{{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}}b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r+t+1}(\widehat{\tau}_{3}^{{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}\otimes b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|+s}(\widehat{\tau}_{2}^{{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}})\otimes\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}}),

where
|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|
denotes the sum of its components.
Then, we have

S_{r,s,t}^{tw}=\sum_{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1},\hbox{\,}{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2},\hbox{\,}{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r+t+1}(id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r}\otimes b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|+s}\otimes id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+t})(\widehat{\tau}_{3}^{{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}\otimes\widehat{\tau}_{2}^{{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}}\otimes\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}}).

Now, consider the following sequence of non-negative integers

{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}=(j_{n},\ldots,j_{t+s+1},j_{t+s}+j^{\prime}_{t+s},j_{t+s-1},\ldots,j_{t+1},j^{\prime}_{t}+j_{t},j_{t-1},\ldots,j_{0}).

Then, we have

S_{r,s,t}^{tw}=\sum_{{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1},{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2},{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}}b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r+t+1}(id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r}\otimes b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|+s}\otimes id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+t})(\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}}),

where
\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}}

is defined using the sequence on non-negative integers
{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}
in a similar way as

\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{\bf{j}_{t}^{1}}
is defined using the sequence
{\bf j}_{t}^{1}
.

For any sequence
{\bf i}_{n}=(i_{n},\ldots,i_{0})
of non-negative integers and any integers
r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
such that
n+|{\bf i}_{n}|=r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime},t^{\prime}\geq 0
and
s^{\prime}\geq 1
, make

S_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}^{{\bf i}_{n}}=b^{ad}_{r^{\prime}+1+t^{\prime}}(id^{\otimes r^{\prime}}\otimes b_{s^{\prime}}^{ad}\otimes id^{\otimes t^{\prime}})(\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf i}_{n}}),

where
\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf i}_{n}}=\delta_{X_{n}}^{\otimes i_{n}}\otimes t_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{X_{1}}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t_{1}\otimes\delta_{X_{0}}^{\otimes i_{0}}
. Hence, we have

S_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}^{{\bf i}_{n}}=b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r+t+1}(id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r}\otimes b^{ad}_{|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|+s}\otimes id^{\otimes|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+t})(\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}}),

for unique integers
r,s,t
, such that
r,t\geq 0
,
s\geq 1
, and
r+s+t=n
, and unique sequences

{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}
,
{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}
,
{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}
with
r^{\prime}=|{\bf{j}}_{r}^{3}|+r
,
s^{\prime}=|{\bf{j}}_{s}^{2}|+s
and

t^{\prime}=|{\bf{j}}_{t}^{1}|+t
, thus
\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf i}_{n}}=\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf j}_{r}^{3}*{\bf j}_{s}^{2}*{\bf j}_{t}^{1}}
.
In fact, we have the following identity

\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}S^{tw}_{r,s,t}=\sum_{{\bf i}_{n}}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}=n+|{\bf i}_{n}|\\
s^{\prime}\geq 1;r^{\prime},t^{\prime}\geq 0\end{matrix}}S_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}^{{\bf i}_{n}}.

Now, since
{\rm ad}(B)
is a
b
-category, for fixed
n
and
i_{n}
, we have

\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}=n+|{\bf i}_{n}|\\
s^{\prime}\geq 1;r^{\prime},t^{\prime}\geq 0\end{matrix}}S_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}^{{\bf i}_{n}}=\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}=n+|{\bf i}_{n}|\\
s^{\prime}\geq 1;r^{\prime},t^{\prime}\geq 0\end{matrix}}b^{ad}_{r^{\prime}+1+t^{\prime}}(id^{\otimes r^{\prime}}\otimes b_{s^{\prime}}^{ad}\otimes id^{\otimes t^{\prime}})(\widehat{\tau}^{{\bf i}_{n}})=0.

Hence, we finally obtain that

\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}S^{tw}_{r,s,t}=0
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The
A_{\infty}
-category
{\rm tw}(A)
can be defined naturally in such a way that
{\rm tw}(A)
is the
A_{\infty}
-category of
{\rm tw}(B)
. Thus,

H^{0}({\rm tw}(A))\simeq H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
.

The following theorem of Keller and Lefèvre-Hasegawa, see
[
10
]
§7, plays an essential role in our later argumentation, which follows closely that of
[
9
]
.

Theorem 6.3
.

If
A
is the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-algebra associated to the
\Lambda
-module
\Delta
, then there is an equivalence of categories

{\cal F}(\Delta)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\simeq}}H^{0}({\rm tw}(A)).

7
The
b
-category
{\rm cv}(B)

In this section
A
is the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-algebra associated to

\Delta
, the algebra
B
denotes its bar construction, and
\hat{D}(B)
its graded dual
S
-
S
-bimodule. The following construction, which was introduced in
[
9
]
, plays an important role in the passage from
H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
to the module category of a special bocs, see §
8
.

Proposition 7.1
.

There is a
b
-category
{\rm cv}(B)
described by the following. The objects of
{\rm cv}(B)
are the left
S
-modules, given two left
S
-modules
X
and
Y
, we have the graded hom space

\begin{matrix}{\rm cv}(B)(X,Y)&=&\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Hom}^{n}_{{\rm GMod}\hbox{-}S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y))\hfill\\
&=&\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Mod}\hbox{-}S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B)_{-n},{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y))\hfill\\
&=&\bigoplus_{n\geq-1}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Mod}\hbox{-}S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

If
n\geq 1
and
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in{\rm Ob}({\rm cv}(B))
,
we have the following homogeneous linear map of degree 1

\begin{matrix}{\rm cv}(B)(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}{\rm cv}(B)(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes_{k}{\rm cv}(B)(X_{0},X_{1})\hbox{\,}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{b^{cv}_{n}}}\hbox{\,}{\rm cv}(B)(X_{0},X_{n})\end{matrix}

which maps each homogeneous generator
F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{2}\otimes F_{1}
on

b_{n}^{cv}(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{2}\otimes F_{1})=(-1)^{\lambda_{n}(|F_{n}|,\ldots,|F_{1}|)}\nu_{n}(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1})\hat{d}(b_{n}),

here we have each
F_{i}:\hat{D}(B)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X_{i-1},X_{i})
, so we have

\hat{D}(B)^{\otimes n}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\ F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}\ }}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes_{S}\cdots\otimes_{S}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X_{0},X_{1})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\nu_{n}}}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X_{0},X_{n}),

where
\nu_{n}
denotes the map induced by composition,
\hat{d}(b_{n})
is defined in (
4.7
), and
\lambda_{n}
is defined in (
2.10
).

Proof.

In order to show that

\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}b^{cv}_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b^{cv}_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1})=0,

we compute each summand
S_{r,s,t}^{cv}:=b^{cv}_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b^{cv}_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}),
where
n=r+s+t
with
s\geq 1
and
t,r\geq 0
. We proceed as we did in the proof that
{\rm tw}(B)
is a
b
-category. Make

\begin{matrix}\phi_{1}^{t}&=&F_{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1},\hfill\\
\phi_{2}^{s}&=&F_{n-r}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{t+1},\hfill\\
\phi^{r}_{3}&=&F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{n-r+1}.\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Then, we have

S^{cv}_{r,s,t}=(-1)^{|\phi_{3}^{r}|}b_{r+1+t}^{cv}(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes b_{s}^{cv}(\phi_{2}^{s})\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})
.
From the equality
|b_{s}^{cv}(\phi_{2}^{s})|=|F_{n-r}|+\cdots+|F_{t+1}|+1
, we obtain

S^{cv}_{r,s,t}=(-1)^{|\phi_{3}^{r}|+u_{1}}\nu_{r+1+t}(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes b^{cv}_{s}(\phi_{2}^{s})\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})\hat{d}(b_{r+1+t}),

where
u_{1}=\lambda_{r+1+t}(|F_{n}|,\ldots,|F_{n-r+1}|,|F_{n-r}|+\cdots+|F_{t+1}|-1,|F_{t}|,\ldots,|F_{1}|)
. Now, make
u_{2}=\lambda_{s}(|F_{n-r}|,\ldots,|F_{t+1}|)
, then we have

S^{cv}_{r,s,t}=(-1)^{|\phi_{3}^{r}|+u_{1}+u_{2}}\nu_{r+1+t}(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes\nu_{s}(\phi_{2}^{s})\hat{d}(b_{s})\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})\hat{d}(b_{r+1+t}),

which coincides with

(-1)^{u}\nu_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes\nu_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes\phi_{2}^{s}\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})(id^{\otimes r}\otimes\hat{d}(b_{s})\otimes id^{\otimes t})\hat{d}(b_{r+1+t}),

where
u=|\phi_{3}^{r}|+u_{1}+u_{2}+|\phi_{1}^{t}|
.

Notice that the map
\lambda_{n}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Z}
, given by

\lambda_{n}(d_{n},\ldots,d_{1})=\sum_{n\geq i>j\geq 1}d_{i}d_{j}
, always satisfies that
\lambda_{n}(d_{n},\ldots,d_{1})
coincides with

\begin{matrix}\lambda_{r+1+t}(d_{n},\ldots,d_{n-r+1},(d_{n-r}+\cdots+d_{n-r-s+1})-1,d_{n-r-s},\ldots,d_{1})\\
+\,\lambda_{s}(d_{n-r},\ldots,d_{n-r-s+1})+\,d_{n}+\cdots+d_{n-r+1}+d_{n-r-s}+\cdots+d_{1}.\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Therefore, we obtain that
u=\lambda_{n}(|F_{n}|,\ldots,|F_{1}|)
. From (
2.13
), we see that the last description of
S_{s,r,t}^{cv}
coincides with

(-1)^{u}\nu_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes\nu_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes\phi_{2}^{s}\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})(id^{\otimes r}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{s}\hat{D}(b_{s})\otimes id^{\otimes t})\hat{\tau}_{r+1+t}\hat{D}(b_{r+1+t}),

and with

(-1)^{u}\nu_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes\nu_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes\phi_{2}^{s}\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})\hat{\tau}_{n}\hat{D}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})\hat{D}(b_{r+1+t}).

Then, we have

\begin{matrix}S_{s,r,t}^{cv}&=&(-1)^{u}\nu_{n}(\phi_{3}^{r}\otimes\phi_{2}^{s}\otimes\phi_{1}^{t})\hat{\tau}_{n}\hat{D}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})\hat{D}(b_{r+1+t})\hfill\\
&=&(-1)^{u}\nu_{n}(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1})\hat{\tau}_{n}\hat{D}(b_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Then, adding up, we obtain

\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}S_{s,r,t}^{cv}=\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}(-1)^{u}\nu_{n}(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1})\hat{\tau}_{n}\hat{D}(b_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})),

where the sum at the right can be rewritten as

(-1)^{u}\nu_{n}(F_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1})\hat{\tau}_{n}\hat{D}(\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r+s+t=n\\
s\geq 1;r,t\geq 0\end{matrix}}b_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes b_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t}))=0,

so
{\rm cv}(B)
is a
b
-category.
∎

Now, we describe an equivalence between
{\rm ad}(B)
and
{\rm cv}(B)
,
which plays an important role in the next section.

Proposition 7.2
.

For each pair of right
S
-modules
X
and
Y
, there is an isomorphism of graded
k
-vector spaces

\Psi_{X,Y}:{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op},Y^{op}).

The recipe of
\Psi
on each homogeneous component of degree
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is the following.
Given

f=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{n}}f_{x}\otimes x\in{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{n}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in{\cal P}}{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j})\otimes_{k}e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}

we have

\Psi(f)\in{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op},Y^{op})_{n}={\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},Y^{op}))
, defined by

\Psi(f)(\zeta)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{n}}f_{x}\zeta(x),

where
\zeta\in\hat{D}(B_{n})
. As before, we consider
\zeta\in\hat{D}(B_{n})\subseteq\hat{D}(B)

and
f_{x}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{s(x)},Ye_{t(x)})\subseteq{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)
.
Moreover, for each sequence of right
S
-modules
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, we have the following commutative diagram

\begin{matrix}{\rm ad}(B)(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}{\rm ad}(B)(X_{0},X_{1})&\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{b^{ad}_{n}}}&{\rm ad}(B)(X_{0},X_{n})\hfill\\
\Bigg\downarrow\hbox to0.0pt{$\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Psi_{X_{n-1},X_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}$}}$\hss}&&\Bigg\downarrow\hbox to0.0pt{$\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Psi_{X_{0},X_{n}}$}}$\hss}\\
{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op}_{n-1},X^{op}_{n})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op}_{0},X^{op}_{1})&\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{b^{cv}_{n}}}&{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op}_{0},X^{op}_{n}).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Proof.

Given any elements
f=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{n}}f_{x}\otimes x\in{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{n}
and
\zeta\in\hat{D}(B_{n})
, the product
f_{x}\zeta(x)
is taken in the
S
-
S
-bimodule
{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)
, but belongs to
{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},Y^{op})={\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)^{op}
. In fact, we have

\begin{matrix}{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op},Y^{op})_{n}&=&{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},Y^{op}))\hfill\\
&=&{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)^{op})\hfill\\
&=&{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(D(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

In order to show that
\Psi(f)\in{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op},Y^{op})_{n}
, we have to verify that

\Psi(f)\in{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(D(B_{n}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)),

so take any

\zeta\in D(B_{n})={\rm Hom}_{S}(\,_{S}B_{n},\,_{S}S)
and let us show that, for any
s,s^{\prime}\in S
, we have

\Psi(f)(s\zeta s^{\prime})=s[\Psi(f)(\zeta)]s^{\prime}
.
It is enough to verify this on any generator
f=f_{x}\otimes x
of
{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{n}
. We have

\begin{matrix}\Psi(f_{x}\otimes x)(s\zeta s^{\prime})&=&f_{x}[(s\zeta s^{\prime})(x)]\hfill&=&f_{x}[\zeta(xs)s^{\prime}]\hfill\\
&=&[f_{x}\zeta(xs)]s^{\prime}\hfill&=&\Psi(f_{x}\otimes xs)(\zeta)s^{\prime}\hfill\\
&=&\Psi(sf_{x}\otimes x)(\zeta)s^{\prime}\hfill&=&sf_{x}\zeta(x)s^{\prime}\hfill\\
&=&s[\Psi(f_{x}\otimes x)(\zeta)]s^{\prime},&\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

because
x\in e_{t(x)}Be_{s(x)}
and
f_{x}\in e_{s(x)}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X,Y)e_{t(x)}
.
So
\Psi_{X,Y}
is a well defined linear map, which restricts to linear maps

{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j})\otimes_{k}e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\Psi_{n}^{i,j}}}{\rm Hom}_{k}(D(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j})).

So, in order to verify that
\Psi_{X,Y}
is an isomorphism, we verify that each
\Psi_{n}^{i,j}
is an isomorphism. For this notice that
D(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i})\cong{\rm Hom}_{k}(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i},k)=D_{k}(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i})
and then we have an isomorphism

{\rm Hom}_{k}(D(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j}))\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\phi}}{\rm Hom}_{k}(D_{k}(e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j})).

Recall that, if
V
,
W
are
k
-vector spaces, there is a natural morphism

\psi:W\otimes_{k}V\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(V^{*},W),

where
V^{*}={\rm Hom}_{k}(V,k)
and
\psi(w\otimes v)(\zeta)=w\zeta(v)
, for
w\in W
,
v\in V
,
\zeta\in V^{*}
. The map
\psi
is an isomorphism when
V
is finite-dimensional. If we take
W={\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j})
and
V=e_{j}B_{n}e_{i}
, we have that
\phi\Psi^{i,j}_{n}=\psi
is an isomorphism. So
\Psi_{X,Y}
is an isomorphism.

Now, take a sequence of right
S
-modules
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.
We want to show that

b_{n}^{cv}(\Psi_{X_{n-1},X_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi_{X_{0},X_{1}})=\Psi_{X_{0},X_{n}}b_{n}^{ad}.

Consider typical homogeneous generators

f_{n}\otimes x_{n}\in{\rm ad}(B)(X_{n-1},X_{n}),\ldots,f_{1}\otimes x_{1}\in{\rm ad}(B)(X_{0},X_{1}),

\zeta\in D(B_{n})
, and let us compute

F_{n}:=b_{n}^{cv}(\Psi_{X_{n-1},X_{n}}(f_{n}\otimes x_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}(f_{1}\otimes x_{1}))(\zeta).

From (
4.14
), we have

\hat{d}(b_{n})(\zeta)=\sum_{z\in\widehat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}}c_{z}z
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
consists of products of the form
z=x^{*}_{i_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{*}_{i_{1}}
and
e_{t(x_{i_{n}})}c_{z}=\zeta(b_{n}(x_{i_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{i_{1}}))
.
Since

F_{n}=(-1)^{\lambda_{n}(|x_{n}|,\ldots,|x_{1}|)}\nu_{n}(\Psi_{X_{n-1},X_{n}}(f_{n}\otimes x_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}(f_{1}\otimes x_{1}))\hat{d}(b_{n})(\zeta),

we obtain

F_{n}=(-1)^{\lambda_{n}(|x_{n}|,\ldots,|x_{1}|)}\sum_{z}c_{z}\nu_{n}(\Psi_{X_{n-1},X_{n}}(f_{n}\otimes x_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes\Psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}(f_{1}\otimes x_{1}))[z].

Hence, we have

\begin{matrix}F_{n}&=&\sum_{x^{*}_{i_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{*}_{i_{1}}}c_{x^{*}_{i_{n}}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{*}_{i_{1}}}f_{n}x_{i_{n}}^{*}(x_{n})\cdots f_{1}x_{i_{1}}^{*}(x_{1})\hfill\\
&=&c_{x^{*}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{*}_{1}}f_{n}\cdots f_{1}\hfill\\
&=&(f_{n}\cdots f_{1})e_{t(x_{n})}c_{x^{*}_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{*}_{1}}\hfill\\
&=&(f_{n}\cdots f_{1})\zeta(b_{n}(x_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}))\hfill\\
&=&\Psi_{X_{0},X_{n}}(f_{n}\cdots f_{1}\otimes b_{n}(x_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}))(\zeta)\hfill\\
&=&\Psi_{X_{0},X_{n}}b_{n}^{ad}((f_{n}\otimes x_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes(f_{1}\otimes x_{1}))(\zeta).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that
t\in{\rm tw}(B)(\underline{X},\underline{Y})_{-1}
, then
t
is a cocycle of the cochain complex
{\rm tw}(B)(\underline{X},\underline{Y})
if and only if, for any
\alpha\in e_{j}\hat{D}(B_{0})e_{i}
,
we have

\Psi(\delta_{Y})(\alpha)\Psi(t)(e^{*}_{i})=\Psi(t)(e^{*}_{j})\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t),

where

S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t)=\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+1=s\\
i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0\end{matrix}}\nu_{s}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes i_{0}})[\delta(\alpha)].

Proof.

We have
t\in{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{-1}
. By definition, the

{\rm tw}(B)
-morphism
t
is a
-1
-cocycle iff we have

b_{1}^{tw}(t)=\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\delta_{Y}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{0}})=0.

From (
7.2
), the preceding formula holds iff the following holds

\begin{matrix}0&=&\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}\Psi b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\delta_{Y}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{0}})\hfill\\
&=&\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}b^{cv}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes i_{0}}).\hfill\\
\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

For any directed element
\alpha\in e_{j}\hat{D}(B_{0})e_{i}
, we have

\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}\nu_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes i_{0}})\hat{d}(b_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1})(\alpha)=0,

or, equivalently,

\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+1=s\\
i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0\end{matrix}}\nu_{s}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes i_{0}})d(\alpha)=0.

From (
4.16
), we have
d(\alpha)=e^{*}_{j}\otimes\alpha-\alpha\otimes e_{i}^{*}+\delta(\alpha)+h(\alpha)
, with
h(\alpha)\in{\cal N}
. Evaluating at this expression of
d(\alpha)
the preceding equation, we obtain

\begin{matrix}0&=&\nu_{2}(\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X}))(e_{j}^{*}\otimes\alpha)-\nu_{2}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})\otimes\Psi(t))(\alpha\otimes e_{i}^{*})+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t)\hfill\\
&=&\nu_{2}(\Psi(t)(e_{j}^{*})\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha))-\nu_{2}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})(\alpha)\otimes\Psi(t)(e_{i}^{*}))+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t),\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

where
S_{\delta(\alpha)}
is defined in the statement of this lemma.
Then, we get

\Psi(\delta_{Y})(\alpha)\Psi(t)(e_{i}^{*})=\Psi(t)(e_{j}^{*})\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t),

as we wanted to show.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

The
b
-category
{\rm tw}(B)
is cohomologically unitary, as in (
5.7
), so
H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))=Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
is a category. Moreover, for each object
\underline{X}
the identity morphism

1_{\underline{X}}
in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))(\underline{X},\underline{X})
of
\underline{X}
satisfies
\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(e_{i}^{*})=id_{Xe_{i}}
, for
i\in{\cal P}
, and
\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(\xi)=0
, for
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
.

Proof.

Take
\underline{X}=(X,\delta_{X})\in{\rm Ob}({\rm tw}(B))
and consider
\tau_{X}:=\sum_{j\in{\cal P}}id_{Xe_{j}}\otimes e_{j}
, thus
\tau_{X}\in{\rm tw}(B)(\underline{X},\underline{X})_{-1}={\rm ad}(B)(X,X)_{-1}
. From (
7.3
), in order to prove that
b_{1}^{tw}(\tau_{X})=0
, we have to show, for

\alpha\in e_{j}\hat{D}(B_{0})e_{i}
, the following equality

\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)\Psi(\tau_{X})(e_{i}^{*})=\Psi(\tau_{X})(e_{j}^{*})\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(\tau_{X}).

Recall that
\Psi(\tau_{X})\in{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{-1}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},X^{op}))
. For
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
, we get

\Psi(\tau_{X})(\xi)=\Psi(\sum_{j\in{\cal P}}id_{Xe_{j}}\otimes e_{j})(\xi)=\sum_{j\in{\cal P}}id_{Xe_{j}}\xi(e_{i})=0,

and, for any
i\in{\cal P}
, we have

\Psi(\tau_{X})(e_{i}^{*})=\Psi(\sum_{j}id_{Xe_{j}}\otimes e_{j})(e_{i}^{*})=id_{Xe_{i}}
.
These equalities imply the wanted equality, because, from (
4.18
), it follows that
S_{\delta(\alpha)}(\tau_{X})=0
. Hence, we have
\tau_{X}\in Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))(\underline{X},\underline{X})
.

Now, given
t\in{\rm tw}(B)(\underline{X},\underline{Y})_{-1}={\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{-1}
, we want to show that
\tau_{Y}t=t
and
t\tau_{X}=t
in
H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))=Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
. That is
b^{tw}(\tau_{Y}\otimes t)=t
and
b^{tw}(t\otimes\tau_{X})=t
.
In order to prove that
t\tau_{X}=t
, we have to show that

\sum_{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0}b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2}(\delta_{Y}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes t\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\tau_{X}\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{0}})=t.

Recall that, for
n\geq 3
, we have
b_{n}(z_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes z_{2}\otimes z_{1})=0
when some factor
z_{i}\in\{e_{j}\mid j\in{\cal P}\}
, see (
4.1
).
Then we are reduced to show that

b_{2}^{ad}(t\otimes\tau_{X})=t
. The last equality holds for the typical generators
f_{s}\otimes x_{s}\in{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{-1}
,
where
x_{s}\in e_{v}B_{-1}e_{u}
and

f_{s}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{u},Ye_{v})
, for some
u,v\in{\cal P}
, because

\begin{matrix}b^{ad}_{2}((f_{s}\otimes x_{s})\otimes\tau_{X})&=&\sum_{j}b^{ad}_{2}((f_{s}\otimes x_{s})\otimes(id_{Xe_{j}}\otimes e_{j}))\hfill\\
&=&\sum_{j}f_{s}id_{Xe_{j}}\otimes b_{2}(x_{s}\otimes e_{j})\hfill\\
&=&f_{s}\otimes((-1)^{1+|x_{s}|}\sigma m_{2}(\sigma^{-1}(x_{s})\otimes\sigma^{-1}(e_{u}))\hfill\\
&=&f_{s}\otimes x_{s}e_{u}=f_{s}\otimes x_{s}.\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Hence
b_{2}^{ad}(t\otimes\tau_{X})=t
, and
t\tau_{X}=t
in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
. Similarly, we have that

b_{2}^{ad}(\tau_{Y}\otimes t)=t
, and then
\tau_{Y}t=t
in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
.
∎

8
The category of
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)
-modules

In this section we describe an equivalence of categories from
H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
onto
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)
-Mod.
We recall from
[
1
]
§2, the following.

Definition 8.1
.

Assume that
{\cal A}={\cal A}(\Delta)
is the weak ditalgebra associated to
\Delta
, which is interlaced with the ideal
I
, as in (
4.19
), see (
9.1
) and (
9.2
).
The category
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is the following.
The class of objects of
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is the class of left
A(\Delta)
-modules

M

such that
IM=0
; given two
A(\Delta)
-modules
M
and
N
annihilated by
I
,
the set of morphisms
{\rm Hom}_{({\cal A},I)}(M,N)
from
M
to
N
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is, by definition,
the
collection of pairs
f=(f^{0},f^{1})
, with
f^{0}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)
and

f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V,{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
, where
V
denotes the bimodule

V=A(\Delta)\otimes_{S}\hat{D}(J)\otimes_{S}A(\Delta)
, such that, for any
a\in A(\Delta)
and
m\in M
,
the following holds

af^{0}[m]=f^{0}[am]+f^{1}(\delta(a))[m].

If
f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V,{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
and

g^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V,{\rm Hom}_{k}(N,L))
, we consider the morphism

g^{1}*f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V^{\otimes 2},{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,L))
defined, for any

\sum_{j}u_{j}\otimes v_{j}\in V\otimes_{A}V
, by

(g^{1}*f^{1})(\sum_{j}u_{j}\otimes v_{j})=\sum_{j}g^{1}(u_{j})f^{1}(v_{j}):M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}L.

Given
f=(f^{0},f^{1})\in{\rm Hom}_{({\cal A},I)}(M,N)
and

g=(g^{0},g^{1})\in{\rm Hom}_{({\cal A},I)}(N,L)
, by definition

gf:=(g^{0}f^{0},(gf)^{1})\in{\rm Hom}_{({\cal A},I)}(M,L)
, where

(gf)^{1}(v):=g^{0}f^{1}(v)+g^{1}(v)f^{0}+(g^{1}*f^{1})(\delta(v)),\hbox{ for }v\in V.

It is convenient to think an
A(\Delta)
-module
M
as a pair
(M,\rho_{M})
, where
M
is a left
S
-module and
\rho_{M}:A(\Delta)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm End}_{k}(M)
is a morphism of
S
-algebras. Moreover, such
\rho_{M}
is uniquely determined by a morphism of
S
-
S
-bimodules, which we denote with the same symbol,

\rho_{M}:\hat{D}(B_{0})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(\,_{S}M,\,_{S}M)
. In the next statement we reformulate the conditions defining
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

We have the following equivalences.

1.

An
A(\Delta)
-module
M
satisfies
IM=0

iff

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\nu_{n}(\rho_{M}^{\otimes n})\hat{d}(b_{n,0})(\zeta)=0,\hbox{ for all }\zeta\in\hat{D}(B)_{-1}.

2.

Given
M,N\in({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, a pair
(f^{0},f^{1})
, where
f^{0}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)
and

f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V,{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
, is a morphism in

({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
iff for any directed element
\alpha\in\hat{D}(B_{0})
, we have

\rho_{N}(\alpha)f_{s(\alpha)}^{0}=f_{t(\alpha)}^{0}\rho_{M}(\alpha)+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1=n}\nu_{n}(\rho_{N}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes f^{1}\otimes\rho_{M}^{\otimes i_{0}})(\delta(\alpha)),

where
f^{0}_{i}=e_{i}f^{0}e_{i}:e_{i}M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}e_{i}N
denotes the restriction of
f^{0}
, for
i\in{\cal P}
.

3.

Given morphisms
f=(f^{0},f^{1}):(M,\rho_{M})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(N,\rho_{N})
and
g=(g^{0},g^{1}):(N,\rho_{N})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(L,\rho_{L})
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, then

(gf)^{1}=g^{0}f^{1}+g^{1}f^{0}-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2=n\\
i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0\end{matrix}}\nu_{n}(\rho_{L}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes g^{1}\otimes\rho_{N}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes f^{1}\otimes\rho_{M}^{\otimes i_{0}})\delta.

Proof.

(1): Given an
A(\Delta)
-module
M
, the action of some element
\alpha\in\hat{D}(B_{0})
on
m\in M
is given by
\alpha m=\rho_{M}(\alpha)(m)
. For an element
\gamma_{s}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes s}
of the form
\gamma_{s}=\alpha_{s}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{1}
, with
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})
, we have

\gamma_{s}m=[\rho_{M}(\alpha_{s})\cdots\rho_{M}(\alpha_{1})](m)=\nu_{s}\rho_{M}^{\otimes s}(\alpha_{s}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{1})(m).

Thus, if
\gamma=\sum_{s}\gamma_{s}
, we have
\gamma m=\sum_{s}\nu_{s}\rho_{M}^{\otimes s}(\gamma_{s})[m]
. Clearly,
IM=0
iff

\beta(\zeta)M=0
, for all
\zeta\in\hat{D}(B)_{-1}
. This is equivalent to

\rho_{M}(\beta(\zeta))=0
. Recall that

\beta(\zeta)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\hat{d}(b_{n,0})(\zeta)
, a finite sum with

\hat{d}(b_{n,0})(\zeta)\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes n}
. So,

\rho_{M}(\beta(\zeta))=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\nu_{n}\rho_{M}^{\otimes n}\hat{d}(b_{n,0})(\zeta)
and (1) holds.

(2): Take
f^{0}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)
and

f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{A(\Delta)\hbox{-}A(\Delta)}(V,{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
. The morphism
f^{1}
is uniquely determined by its restriction
f^{1}:\hat{D}(J)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N)
, by the formula

f^{1}(\gamma_{r}\otimes\xi\otimes\gamma_{t})=(\nu_{r}\rho_{N}^{\otimes r}\gamma_{r})f^{1}(\xi)(\nu_{t}\rho_{M}^{\otimes t}\gamma_{t})=\nu_{r+1+t}(\rho_{N}^{\otimes r}\otimes f^{1}\otimes\rho_{M}^{\otimes t})(\gamma_{r}\otimes\xi\otimes\gamma_{t}),

for
\gamma_{r}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes r}
,
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
and
\gamma_{t}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes t}
. The condition for
(f^{0},f^{1})
to be a morphism from
M
to
N
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is equivalent to

\alpha f^{0}[m]=f^{0}[\alpha m]+f^{1}(\delta(\alpha))[m],

for any
\alpha\in\hat{D}(B_{0})
and
m\in M
. We can write
\delta(\alpha)=\sum_{n}\delta(\alpha)_{n}
, where

\delta(\alpha)_{n}=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{2}\otimes\xi_{i}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{1},

with
\gamma_{i}^{2}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes r}
,
\xi_{i}\in\hat{D}(J)
,
\gamma_{i}^{1}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes t}
satisfying that

r+1+t=n
. Then, we have

f^{1}(\delta(\alpha))=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{r+1+t=n}\nu_{n}(\rho_{N}^{\otimes r}\otimes f^{1}\otimes\rho_{M}^{\otimes t})(\delta(\alpha)).

Then, (2) holds.

(3): Consider morphisms
f=(f^{0},f^{1}):(M,\rho_{M})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(N,\rho_{N})
and
g=(g^{0},g^{1}):(N,\rho_{N})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(L,\rho_{L})
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Given
v\in V
, we can write
\delta(v)=\sum_{n}\delta(v)_{n}
, where

\delta(v)_{n}=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}^{3}\otimes\xi_{i}^{2}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{2}\otimes\xi_{i}^{1}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{1},

with
\gamma_{i}^{3}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes r}
,
\gamma_{i}^{2}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes s}
,
\gamma_{i}^{1}\in\hat{D}(B_{0})^{\otimes t}
, and
\xi^{1}_{i},\xi_{i}^{2}\in\hat{D}(J)
, satisfying that
r+s+t+2=n
. Then, since the morphism
f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(J),{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
has degree 1, we obtain

(g^{1}*f^{1})(\delta(v))=-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{r+s+t+2=n}\nu_{n}(\rho_{L}^{\otimes r}\otimes g^{1}\otimes\rho_{N}^{\otimes s}\otimes f^{1}\otimes\rho_{M}^{\otimes t})(\delta(v)).

Then, we get the formula in (3).
∎

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
A
be the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-algebra associated to
\Delta
,
B
its bar construction, and
{\cal A}=({\cal A}(\Delta),\delta)
the corresponding weak ditalgebra with the ideal
I
fixed in (
4.17
). Then, there is an equivalence of categories

H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))\simeq({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}.

Since
{\rm tw}(B)(\underline{X},\underline{Y})_{n}={\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{n}=0
, for
n<-1
, the category
H^{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
coincides with the category

Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
which has the same objects than
{\rm tw}(B)
, and the morphisms
t:\underline{X}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{Y}

of
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
are the elements in
{\rm ad}(B)(X,Y)_{-1}
such that

b_{1}^{tw}(t)=\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\delta_{Y}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{0}})=0.

The composition of two morphisms
t_{1}:\underline{X}_{0}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{X}_{1}
and
t_{2}:\underline{X}_{1}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{X}_{2}

in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
is given by

t_{2}t_{1}=b_{2}^{tw}(t_{2}\otimes t_{1})=\sum_{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0}b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2}(\delta_{X_{2}}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes t_{2}\otimes\delta_{X_{1}}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t_{1}\otimes\delta_{X_{0}}^{\otimes i_{0}}).

The equivalence functor
M:Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is defined as follows. Given
\underline{X}\in Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
, its image is the
A(\Delta)
-module
M(\underline{X})=(X^{op},\Psi(\delta_{X}))
, where
\Psi
is the isomorphism introduced in (
7.2
). Given a morphism
t:\underline{X}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{Y}

in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
, its image
M(t)=(M(t)^{0},M(t)^{1}):M(\underline{X})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M(\underline{Y})
is given by

M(t)^{0}=\Psi(t)(\sum_{j\in{\cal P}}e^{*}_{j})\hbox{ \ and \ }M(t)^{1}(\xi)=\Psi(t)(\xi),\hbox{ for }\xi\in\hat{D}(J).

Proof.

Step 1:
According to (
8.2
), in order to show that
IM(\underline{X})=0
, we have to prove that, for any
\zeta\in\hat{D}(B)_{-1}
, we have
\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\nu_{s}(\rho_{M}^{\otimes s})\hat{d}(b_{s,0})(\zeta)=0
. But, since
\underline{X}
is an object of
{\rm tw}(B)
, it satisfies
\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}b_{s}^{ad}(\delta_{X}^{\otimes s})=0
. From the commutativity of the square in (
7.2
), using (
4.12
) and the fact that
|\delta_{X}|=0
, we obtain

\begin{matrix}0&=&\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\Psi b_{s}^{ad}(\delta_{X}^{\otimes s})(\zeta)\hfill\\
&=&\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}b^{cv}_{s}(\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes s})(\zeta)\hfill\\
&=&\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\nu_{s}(\rho_{M}^{\otimes s})\hat{d}(b_{s})(\zeta)\hfill\\
&=&\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\nu_{s}(\rho_{M}^{\otimes s})\hat{d}(b_{s,0})(\zeta).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

So, indeed we have
M(\underline{X})\in({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.

Step 2:
Take a morphism
t:\underline{X}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{Y}

of
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
.
From (
7.3
), we have

\Psi(\delta_{Y})(\alpha)\Psi(t)(e^{*}_{i})=\Psi(t)(e^{*}_{j})\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t),

for any
\alpha\in e_{j}\hat{D}(B_{0})e_{i}
, where

S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t)=\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+1=s\\
i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0\end{matrix}}\nu_{s}(\Psi(\delta_{Y})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t)\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes i_{0}})(\delta(\alpha)).

We want to show that the pair
M(t)=(M(t)^{0},M(t)^{1})
satisfies the equation of (
8.2
)(2),
where
M(t)^{1}
denotes also the extension of the morphism

M(t)^{1}:\hat{D}(J)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(M(\underline{X}),M(\underline{Y}))
to
V
.

We have that
\Psi(t)\in{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B_{-1}),{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},Y^{op}))
. For each
i\in{\cal P}
, we have
M(t)^{0}_{i}=e_{i}M(t)^{0}e_{i}=e_{i}\Psi(t)(\sum_{j}e_{j}^{*})e_{i}=\Psi(t)(e_{i}^{*})
. Then, we get

\Psi(\delta_{Y})(\alpha)M(t)^{0}_{i}=M(t)_{j}^{0}\Psi(\delta_{X})(\alpha)+S_{\delta(\alpha)}(t),

which means that
M(t):M(\underline{X})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M(\underline{Y})
is a morphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, according to
(
8.2
)(2).

Step 3:
Take morphisms
t_{1}:\underline{X}_{0}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{X}_{1}
and
t_{2}:\underline{X}_{1}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{X}_{2}

in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
, and let us show that the given association rule
M
preserves their composition:
M(t_{2}t_{1})=M(t_{2})M(t_{1})
.
Applying
\Psi
to the formula of the composition
t_{2}t_{1}
in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
recalled in the statement of this theorem, we have

\Psi(t_{2}t_{1})=\sum_{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0}\Psi(b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2}(\delta_{X_{2}}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes t_{2}\otimes\delta_{X_{1}}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t_{1}\otimes\delta_{X_{0}}^{\otimes i_{0}})),

which coincides with

\sum_{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0}b^{cv}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2}(\Psi(\delta_{X_{2}})^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes\Psi(t_{2})\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X_{1}})^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t_{1})\otimes\Psi(\delta_{X_{0}})^{\otimes i_{0}}).

For simplicity, we write
M_{u}
and
\rho_{u}
instead of
M(\underline{X}_{u})
and
\Psi(\delta_{X_{u}})
, respectively, for
u\in\{0,1,2\}
. Then, for a basic directed element
x\in e_{j}\mathbb{B}_{-1}e_{i}
, we have that
\Psi(t_{2}t_{1})(x^{*})
coincides with

\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2=s\\
i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0\end{matrix}}(-1)^{u(i_{2},i_{1},i_{0})}\nu_{s}(\rho_{2}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes\Psi(t_{2})\otimes\rho_{1}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t_{1})\otimes\rho_{0}^{\otimes i_{0}})d(x^{*}),

with
u(i_{2},i_{1},i_{0})=\lambda_{s}(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)=1
, where the first sequence of ceros, from left to right, has length
i_{2}
, the second has length
i_{1}
, and the third one has length
i_{0}
.

If
x=e_{i}
, then
d(e_{i}^{*})=e^{*}_{i}\otimes e^{*}_{i}+h(e^{*}_{i})
, with
h(e_{i}^{*})\in{\cal N}
. It follows that

M(t_{2}t_{1})^{0}_{i}=\Psi(t_{2}t_{1})(e_{i}^{*})=-\nu_{2}(\Psi(t_{2})\otimes\Psi(t_{1}))(e^{*}_{i}\otimes e^{*}_{i})=\Psi(t_{2})(e_{i}^{*})\Psi(t_{1})(e^{*}_{i})=M(t_{2})^{0}_{i}M(t_{1})_{i}^{0}
, for all
i\in{\cal P}
, so
M(t_{2}t_{1})^{0}=M(t_{2})^{0}M(t_{1})^{0}
.

If
x\in J
, we have

d(x^{*})=e_{j}^{*}\otimes x^{*}+x^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*}+\delta(x^{*})+h(x^{*})
, with
h(x^{*})\in{\cal N}
. Then, we have
that
M(t_{2}t_{1})^{1}(x^{*})=\Psi(t_{2}t_{1})(x^{*})=S_{0}+S_{\delta(x^{*})}
, where

S_{0}=-\nu_{2}(\Psi(t_{2})\otimes\Psi(t_{1}))(e_{j}^{*}\otimes x^{*}+x^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*}),\hbox{ and }

S_{\delta(x^{*})}=-\sum_{s=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}i_{0}+i_{1}+i_{2}+2=s\\
i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0\end{matrix}}\nu_{s}(\rho_{2}^{\otimes i_{2}}\otimes\Psi(t_{2})\otimes\rho_{1}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes\Psi(t_{1})\otimes\rho_{0}^{\otimes i_{0}})(\delta(x^{*})).

Moreover, we have

\begin{matrix}S_{0}&=&\Psi(t_{2})(e_{j}^{*})\Psi(t_{1})(x^{*})+\Psi(t_{2})(x^{*})\Psi(t_{1})(e_{i}^{*})\hfill\\
&=&M(t_{2})^{0}_{j}M(t_{1})^{1}(x^{*})+M(t_{2})^{1}(x^{*})M(t_{1})^{0}_{i}\hfill\\
&=&[M(t_{2})^{0}M(t_{1})^{1}+M(t_{2})^{1}M(t_{1})^{0}](x^{*}).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

By the definition of the composition in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, we have

(M(t_{2})M(t_{1}))^{1}=M(t_{2})^{0}M(t_{1})^{1}+M(t_{2})^{1}M(t_{1})^{0}+(M(t_{2})^{1}*M(t_{1})^{1})\delta.

So, we only have to verify that
(M(t_{2})^{1}*M(t_{1})^{1})(\delta(x^{*}))=S_{\delta(x^{*})}
.
But, according with (
8.2
)(3), we have this equality. Hence, we get that
M
preserves composition of morphisms.

According with (
7.4
), the identity morphism

1_{\underline{X}}
in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))(\underline{X},\underline{X})
of the object
\underline{X}
satisfies
\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(e_{i}^{*})=id_{Xe_{i}}
and
\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(\xi)=0
, for all
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
. Then, we have

M(1_{\underline{X}})^{0}_{i}=\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(e_{i}^{*})=id_{Xe_{i}}
and,
if
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
, we have
M(1_{\underline{X}})^{1}(\xi)=\Psi(1_{\underline{X}})(\xi)=0
.
So
M
preserves identities and we have a
functor
M:Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.

Step 4:
Let us show that the functor
M
is dense. So, consider an object in
(N,\rho_{N})\in({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Then, we can write it as
N=X^{op}
, where
X
is a right
S
-module.
We have

\rho_{N}\in{\rm Hom}_{S\hbox{-}S}(\hat{D}(B)_{0},{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},X^{op}))
, but we know that

\Psi:{\rm ad}(B)(X,X)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm cv}(B)(X^{op},X^{op})
is an isomorphism, so there is some
\delta_{X}\in{\rm ad}(B)(X,X)
homogeneous with degree 0 such that
\Psi(\delta_{X})=\rho_{N}
.

By (
4.3
), we have that
\hat{D}(B)_{0}
is finite dimensional and
\hat{D}(B)_{0}^{\otimes\ell}=0
, then the
k
-algebra
\Gamma=A(\Delta)=T_{S}(\hat{D}(B_{0}))
is finite dimensional. Consider the Jacobson radical
{\rm rad}(\Gamma)
of
\Gamma
, so
we have
{\rm rad}(\Gamma)^{n}=0
for its nilpotence index
n
. Consider the filtration of left
S
-modules

0={\rm rad}(\Gamma)^{n}N\subseteq\cdots\subseteq{\rm rad}(\Gamma)^{2}N\subseteq{\rm rad}(\Gamma)N\subseteq N

or, equivalently, the filtration of right
S
-modules

0=X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{n}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{2}\subseteq X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})\subseteq X.

If we write
\delta_{X}=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{0}}f_{x}\otimes x
and take a directed basic element
y\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, we obtain

\rho_{N}(y^{*})=\Psi(\delta_{X})(y^{*})=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}_{0}}f_{x}y^{*}(x)=f_{y}e_{t(y)}=f_{y}
. Since
y^{*}\in\hat{D}(B)_{0}\subseteq{\rm rad}(\Gamma)
, it annihilates the semisimple right
\Gamma^{op}
-module
X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{s}/X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{s+1}
, for all
s\in[0,n-1]
. Hence, we have

f_{y}(X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{s})=\rho_{N}(y^{*})X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{s}\subseteq X{\rm rad}(\Gamma^{op})^{s+1},

and the pair
(X,\delta_{X})
satisfies the first condition of (
6.1
).
For the second one, since
(N,\rho_{N})\in({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, the
A(\Delta)
-module
N
satisfies

IN=0
. As we saw in (
8.2
), this means that

\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\nu_{n}(\rho_{N}^{\otimes n})\hat{d}(b_{n,0})=0
. Then, we have

0=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}b^{cv}_{n}(\Psi(\delta_{X})^{\otimes n})=\Psi(\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}b_{n}^{ad}(\delta_{X}^{\otimes n})).

Since
\Psi
is an isomorphism, we obtain that
\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}b_{n}^{ad}(\delta_{X}^{\otimes n})=0
. Therefore, the pair
(X,\delta_{X})
is an object of
{\rm tw}(B)

Step 5:
Let us show that
M
is a faithful and full functor. Assume that
t:\underline{X}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\underline{Y}
is a morphism in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
such that
M(t)=0
. Thus, we have
\Psi(t)(e_{i}^{*})=M(t)^{0}_{i}=0
, for
i\in{\cal P}
, and
\Psi(t)(\xi)=M(t)^{1}(\xi)=0
, for
\xi\in\hat{D}(J)
. Here, the domain of
\Psi(t)
is
\hat{D}(B)_{1}=\bigoplus_{j\in{\cal P}}ke_{j}^{*}\oplus\hat{D}(J)
, so we get
\Psi(t)=0
. Since
\Psi
is an isomorphism we obtain
t=0
, and
M
is a faithful functor.

Let
f=(f^{0},f^{1}):M(X,\delta_{X})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M(Y,\delta_{Y})
be a morphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Consider the morphism of
S
-
S
-bimodules
\phi:\hat{D}(B)_{1}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\rm Hom}_{k}(X^{op},Y^{op})
defined by

\phi(e_{i}^{*})=e_{i}f^{0}e_{i}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{i})\hbox{ \ and \ }\phi(\xi)=e_{j}f^{1}(\xi)e_{i}\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(Xe_{i},Ye_{j}),

for
i\in{\cal P}
and
\xi\in e_{j}Je_{i}\cap\mathbb{B}
. Since
\Psi
is surjective, there is some morphism
t\in{\rm ad}(B)(X^{op},Y^{op})_{-1}
such that
\Psi(t)=\phi
. Since
f=(f^{0},f^{1})
is a morphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, we have the equation
(
8.2
)(2), for all directed elements
\alpha\in\hat{D}(B)_{0}
. Then, we can reverse the argument given in
Step 2
to obtain that
t
satisfies

b_{1}^{tw}(t)=\sum_{i_{0},i_{1}\geq 0}b^{ad}_{i_{0}+i_{1}+1}(\delta_{Y}^{\otimes i_{1}}\otimes t\otimes\delta_{X}^{\otimes i_{0}})=0.

So,
t:(X,\delta_{X})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(Y,\delta_{Y})
is a morphism in
Z_{-1}({\rm tw}(B))
. Then, we have that
M
is a full functor.
∎

9
The weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}(\Delta)
and the ideal
I

We recall some terminology from
[
3
]
and
[
1
]
.

Definition 9.1
.

A
weak ditalgebra

{\cal A}
is a pair
{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
, where
T
is a graded tensor algebra and
\delta
is a
differential
on
T
, that is
\delta:T\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}T
is a homogeneous linear map of degree 1 and satisfies Leibniz rule

\delta(ab)=\delta(a)b+(-1)^{|a|}a\delta(b)

on homogeneous elements
a,b\in T
.
The weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}
is called a
ditalgebra
if, furthermore, we have
\delta^{2}=0
.

A weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
has
layer
(R,W)
iff
R
is a
k
-algebra and
W
is an
R
-
R
-bimodules equipped with an
R
-
R
-bimodule decomposition
W_{0}\oplus W_{1}
such that
W_{0}\subseteq T_{0}
,
W_{1}\subseteq T_{1}
, the algebra
T
is freely generated by the pair
(R,W)
, and
\delta(R)=0
, see
[
3
]
(4.1). When there is no danger of confusion, we write
A:=T_{0}
and
V:=T_{1}
. Thus, we have
A\cong T_{R}(W_{0})
and
V\cong A\otimes_{R}W_{1}\otimes_{R}A
.

A layer
(R,W)
of a weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
is called

triangular
if

1.

There is a filtration of
R
-
R
-subbimodules

0=W_{0}^{0}\subseteq W_{0}^{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq W_{0}^{r}=W_{0}
such that

\delta(W_{0}^{i+1})\subseteq A_{i}W_{1}A_{i}
, for all
i\in[0,r-1]
,
where
A_{i}
denotes the
R
-subalgebra
A
generated by
W_{0}^{i}
.

2.

There is a filtration of
R
-
R
-subbimodules

0=W_{1}^{0}\subseteq W_{1}^{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq W_{1}^{s}=W_{1}
such that

\delta(W_{1}^{i+1})\subseteq AW_{1}^{i}AW_{1}^{i}A
, for all
i\in[0,s-1]
.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
be a weak ditalgebra, make
A=T_{0}
and
V=T_{1}
.
Assume that
I
is an ideal of
A
, satisfying that
\delta(I)\subseteq IV+VI
.
Then, the weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}
is called
interlaced with the ideal
I

iff

1.

\delta^{2}(A)\subseteq IV^{2}+VIV+V^{2}I
, and

2.

\delta^{2}(V)\subseteq IV^{3}+VIV^{2}+V^{2}IV+V^{3}I
.

The pair
({\cal A},I)
is called then an
interlaced weak
ditalgebra
.

Definition 9.3
.

Let
{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
be a weak ditalgebra, make
A=T_{0}
and
V=T_{1}
, and assume that

I
is an ideal of
A
. Then,
we say that
I
is an

{\cal A}
-triangular ideal of
A
iff there is a sequence of

k
-subspaces

0=H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{t}=I

such that

\delta(H_{i})\subseteq AH_{i-1}V+VH_{i-1}A,\hbox{ for all }i\in[1,t].

Definition 9.4
.

A pair
({\cal A},I)
is called a
triangular interlaced weak ditalgebra
if

{\cal A}=(T,\delta)
is a weak ditalgebra interlaced with the ideal
I
of
T_{0}
, where
{\cal A}
has a triangular layer
and
I
is an
{\cal A}
-triangular ideal.

The importance of these triangular interlaced weak ditalgebras
({\cal A},I)
is that the reduction procedures introduced by the Kiev school of representation theory can be applied to the study of their module categories
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, see
[
1
]
.
In order to show that the pair
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)
constructed in §4, see (
4.19
), is a triangular interlaced weak ditalgebra, we need some preliminary facts.

Remark 9.5
.

We will use a basis
\mathbb{B}
of
B
as constructed in (
4.9
), but we refine the choice of the basis of the vector space
J
as follows. Sine
J
is nilpotent, we have a filtration

0=J^{\ell_{0}}\subset\cdots\subset J^{2}\subset J
, and an
S
-
S
-bimodule decomposition
J=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{\ell_{0}-1}
, such that

J^{u}=J^{(u+1)}\oplus L_{u}
, for all
u\in[1,\ell_{0}-1]
. Then, we can choose directed
k
-vector space basis
\mathbb{J}_{u}(i,j)\subseteq e_{j}L_{u}e_{i}
, for
i,j\in{\cal P}
and
u\in[1,\ell_{0}-1]
. Then, we make
\mathbb{J}_{u}=\bigcup_{i,j}\mathbb{J}_{u}(i,j)
and
\mathbb{J}=\bigcup_{u}\mathbb{J}_{u}
. For the construction of
\mathbb{B}
in (
4.9
), we use this special choice for the basis of
J
.

As usual, the
depth
\nu_{1}(x)
of an element
x\in J
is the number such that
x\in J^{\nu_{1}(x)}\setminus J^{\nu_{1}(x)+1}
. Here, the set of basic elements in
x\in\mathbb{J}
with
\nu_{1}(x)=u
is precisely
\mathbb{J}_{u}
. Thus, for
x\in\mathbb{J}
, we have that
x^{*}(L_{v})=0
, for all
v\not=\nu_{1}(x)
.
If
x\in\mathbb{J}
, we make
\nu_{1}(x^{*})=\nu_{1}(x)
.

We will also refine the choice of the basis
\mathbb{B}_{0}
as follows. For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the
S
-
S
-subbimodule
U_{t}:=\sum_{r+s=t}J^{r}B_{0}J^{s}
of
B_{0}
. Then, we have an
S
-
S
-bimodule filtration

0=U_{\ell_{1}}\subset\cdots\subset U_{1}\subset U_{0}=B_{0}
, and an
S
-
S
-bimodule decomposition

B_{0}=G_{0}\oplus G_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus G_{\ell_{1}-1}
, with
U_{v}=U_{v+1}\oplus G_{v}
, for all
v\in[1,\ell_{1}-1]
.
As before, we choose directed
k
-vector space basis
\mathbb{G}_{v}(i,j)\subseteq e_{j}G_{v}e_{i}
, for
i,j\in{\cal P}

and
v\in[1,\ell_{1}-1]
. Then, we make
\mathbb{G}_{v}=\bigcup_{i,j}\mathbb{G}_{v}(i,j)
and
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\bigcup_{v}\mathbb{G}_{v}
. For the construction of
\mathbb{B}
in (
4.9
), we use this special choice for the basis of
B_{0}
.

As before, the
depth
\nu_{0}(x)
of an element
x\in B_{0}
is the number such that
x\in U_{\nu_{0}(x)}\setminus U_{\nu_{0}(x)+1}
. Thus, for
x\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, we have that
x^{*}(G_{v})=0
if
v\not=\nu_{0}(x)
. Also, for
x\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
, we make
\nu_{0}(x^{*})=\nu_{0}(x)
.

Remark 9.6
.

Notice that the pair
(S,W)
, where
W_{0}=\hat{D}(B)_{0}
and
W_{1}=\hat{D}(J)
, is a layer for the weak ditalgebra
{\cal A}(\Delta)=(T(\Delta),\delta)
, see (
4.19
).

Let us consider
the bigraph
{\cal B}
of the layered ditalgebra
{\cal A}(\Delta)
,
it has set of points
{\cal P}
; given
i,j\in{\cal P}
,
the set of
solid arrows
from
i
to
j
is
\mathbb{B}^{*}_{0}(i,j)
and
the set of
dashed arrows
from
i
to
j
is
\mathbb{J}^{*}(i,j)
.
The starting point of any path
\gamma
is denoted by
s(\gamma)
and its terminal point by
t(\gamma)
. A path
\gamma
is called a
cycle
iff
s(\gamma)=t(\gamma)
. A cycle
\gamma
is called a
loop
iff
\gamma
has only one arrow.

Notice that the graded tensor algebra
T(\Delta)=T_{S}(W_{0}\oplus W_{1})
can be identified with the
path algebra
k({\cal B})
of
the bigraph
{\cal B}
, whose underlying vector space has as basis the set of
paths (of any kind of arrows) of
{\cal B}
(including one trivial path for
each point
i\in{\cal P}
) and the product is induced by the concatenation of paths. Each idempotent
e_{i}
of
S
is
identified with the trivial path at the point
i
.
The homogeneous elements
h\in k({\cal B})_{u}
of
degree

u
are the linear combinations of paths containing exactly
u
dashed arrows.

We visualize the products of basic directed elements of
\mathbb{B}^{*}_{0}\cup\mathbb{J}^{*}
in
T(\Delta)
as paths of the bigraph
{\cal B}
.
An important observation is the following.

1.

If
\alpha:i\rightarrow j
is solid arrow of
{\cal B}
,
we have
\overline{i}<\overline{j}
in the quotient poset
\overline{\cal P}
.

2.

If
\xi:i\rightarrow j
is a dashed arrow of
{\cal B}
,
we have
\overline{i}\leq\overline{j}
in the quotient poset
\overline{\cal P}
.

Lemma 9.7
.

A path
\gamma
from
i
to
j
in the bigraph
{\cal B}
is called a
precycle
iff
i\sim j
. A precycle of length 1 is called a
preloop
. Then, any precycle
\gamma
of length
\geq 1
consists of dashed arrows only.

Proof.

Assume that
\gamma=\alpha_{n}\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}
is a precycle from
i
to
j
, where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
are (solid or dashed) arrows of
{\cal B}
, with
t(\alpha_{u})=i_{u}
, for
u\in[1,n]
. Then, by the last observation in (
9.6
), we have

\overline{i}\leq\overline{i_{1}}\leq\cdots\leq\overline{i_{n}}=\overline{j}
with
\overline{i}=\overline{j}
, thus
\overline{i}=\overline{i_{1}}=\cdots=\overline{i_{n}}
. Then all the arrows
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
must be dashed arrows.
∎

In the following we use the height maps
h:X\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
associated to various finite posets
(X,\leq)
, as defined in (
1.11
). Recall that every height map
h
satisfies:
y<x
implies
h(y)<h(x)
, for all
x,y\in X
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Consider the quotient poset
\overline{\cal P}
associated to
{\cal P}
, and its height map
h:\overline{\cal P}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Consider the set of paths
\Gamma
of
{\cal B}
and the map
d:\Gamma\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
defined, for each path
\gamma
in
{\cal B}
, as
d(\gamma)=h(\overline{t(\gamma)})-h(\overline{s(\gamma)})
.
We have the following.

1.

If
\alpha
is a solid arrow, then
d(\alpha)>0
.

2.

If
\xi
is a dashed arrow, then
d(\xi)\geq 0
and (
d(\xi)=0
iff
\xi
is a preloop).

3.

Whenever
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a
factorization of the path
\gamma
as a product of non-trivial subpaths, where
exactly one of them is a precycle, we have

d(\gamma)=d(\eta),\hbox{ where }\eta\in\{\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\}\hbox{ is not a precycle.}

4.

Whenever
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a
factorization of the path
\gamma
as a product of non-trivial subpaths, where
at most one of them is a precycle, we have

d(\gamma)>d(\eta),\hbox{ for each }\eta\in\{\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\}\hbox{ not a precycle.}

Proof.

(1), (2) and (3) are clear. In (4), we have three cases. Assume, for instance, that
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
is the only precycle. Then, we have

\overline{s(\gamma)}=\overline{s(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime})}=\overline{t(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime})}=\overline{s(\gamma^{\prime\prime})}<\overline{t(\gamma^{\prime\prime})}=\overline{s(\gamma^{\prime})}<\overline{t(\gamma^{\prime})}=\overline{t(\gamma)},

thus
d(\gamma^{\prime})<d(\gamma)
and
d(\gamma^{\prime\prime})<d(\gamma)
. The other cases are similar.
∎

Proposition 9.9
.

The pair
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)
constructed in §4, see (
4.19
), is a triangular interlaced weak ditalgebra.

Proof.

We adopt the preceding notations.

Step 1: Description of the triangular filtration of
W_{0}
, see (
9.1
)(1).

Consider the partial order on the set
{\cal P}(\mathbb{B}^{*}_{0})=\{(d(\alpha),\nu_{0}(\alpha))\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{B}^{*}_{0}\}

defined by
(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
iff
i<i^{\prime}
or (if
i=i^{\prime}
, then
j<j^{\prime}
), and its corresponding height map
h_{0}:{\cal P}(\mathbb{B}^{*}_{0})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Write

h_{0}(\alpha):=h_{0}(d(\alpha),\nu_{0}(\alpha))
, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{B}_{0}^{*}
. Thus,
(d(\alpha),\nu_{0}(\alpha))<(d(\beta),\nu_{0}(\beta))
implies that
h_{0}(\alpha)<h_{0}(\beta)
.

Consider the filtration

0=W_{0}^{0}\subseteq W_{0}^{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq W_{0}^{r}=W_{0}
,
where
W_{0}^{u}
is the
S
-
S
-subbimodule of
W_{0}
generated
by
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{B}_{0}^{*}\mid h_{0}(\alpha)<u\}
.

Assume that we have a solid arrow
\alpha\in W_{0}^{u}
, thus
h_{0}(\alpha)<u
and
u\geq 1
. We want to show that
\delta(\alpha)\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
. We can write

\delta(\alpha)=\delta(\alpha)_{2}+\delta(\alpha)_{3}+\cdots+\delta(\alpha)_{w},

for some
w\geq 2
and each
\delta(\alpha)_{v}
is a linear combination of paths of length
v
and degree 1.
Recall that
\alpha
is a solid arrow, so it can not be a cycle.

Then, if
\delta(\alpha)_{v}\not=0
, for
v\in[3,w]
, there is a path (non-cycle)

\gamma_{2}\xi\gamma_{1}
in its expression as linear combination of paths, with non-zero coefficient, where
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}
are paths composed by solid arrows and
\xi
is a dashed arrow. Here, from (
4.18
), we know that one of
\gamma_{1}
or
\gamma_{2}
must be non-trivial. Then, (
9.8
) implies that whenever
\gamma_{1}
is not trivial (resp.
\gamma_{2}
non-trivial) we have
d(\gamma_{1})<d(\gamma_{2}\xi\gamma_{1})=d(\alpha)
(resp.
d(\gamma_{2})<d(\gamma_{2}\xi\gamma_{1})=d(\alpha)
), then, in any case, the arrows
\beta
composing
\gamma_{1}
or
\gamma_{2}
satisfy
d(\beta)<d(\alpha)
, so
h_{0}(\beta)<h_{0}(\alpha)
, which implies that
\gamma_{2}\xi\gamma_{1}\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
. So
\delta(\alpha)_{v}\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
.

It remains to consider the case
v=2
. If
\delta(\alpha)_{2}\not=0
, there is a path of the form
\xi\beta
(or
\beta\xi
), where
\beta
is a solid arrow and
\xi
is a dashed arrow, appearing in the expression of
\delta(\alpha)_{2}

with non-zero coefficient. If
\xi
is not a preloop, we proceed as before to
obtain that

\beta\xi\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
(or
\xi\beta\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
). Assume that
\xi
is a preloop and that the path
\xi\beta
has appeared in
\delta(\alpha)_{2}
. Here, we have
d(\beta)=d(\alpha)
. Let us examine its coefficient
c^{\alpha}_{\xi\otimes\beta}
in the explicit description of
\delta(\alpha)
given in (
4.18
).
We have
\alpha=x^{*}
,
\beta=y^{*}
and
\xi=r^{*}
, where
x,y\in\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
r\in\mathbb{J}
, then

0\not=e_{t(\alpha)}c^{\alpha}_{\xi\otimes\beta}=x^{*}[b_{2}(r\otimes y)]=x^{*}[\sigma(\sigma^{-1}(r)\sigma^{-1}(y))]=x^{*}(ry).

We know that
r\in\mathbb{J}
, thus
r\in\mathbb{J}_{s}\subseteq J^{s}
, for some
s\in[1,\ell_{0}-1]
. Thus, we get
ry\in U_{s+\nu_{0}(y)}=\bigoplus_{l\geq s+\nu_{0}(y)}G_{l}
. Since
x^{*}(ry)\not=0
, we have
x^{*}(U_{s+\nu_{0}(y)})\not=0
, thus
x^{*}(G_{l})\not=0
, for some
l\geq s+\nu_{0}(y)
.
This implies that

\nu_{0}(\beta)=\nu_{0}(y)<s+\nu_{0}(y)\leq l=\nu_{0}(x)=\nu_{0}(\alpha)
,
so
h_{0}(\beta)<h_{0}(\alpha)
and
\xi\beta\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
.
The case where
\beta\xi
appears in the expression of
\delta(\alpha)
with non-zero coefficient is treated similarly.
Then, we get
\delta(\alpha)_{2}\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
. Adding up, we get that
\delta(\alpha)\in A_{u-1}\hat{D}(J)A_{u-1}
, and we have the triangularity condition for
W_{0}
.

Step 2: Description of the triangular filtration of
W_{1}
, see (
9.1
)(2).

Consider the partial order on the set
{\cal P}(\mathbb{J}^{*})=\{(d(\xi),\nu_{1}(\xi))\mid\xi\in\mathbb{J}^{*}\}
defined by
(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
iff
i<i^{\prime}
or (if
i=i^{\prime}
, then
j<j^{\prime}
). The poset

{\cal P}(\mathbb{J}^{*})
admits a height map
h_{1}:{\cal P}(\mathbb{J}^{*})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Write
h_{1}(\xi)=h_{1}(d(\xi),\nu_{1}(\xi))
, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{J}^{*}
. Thus, as before,
(d(\xi),\nu_{1}(\xi))<(d(\zeta),\nu_{1}(\zeta))
implies that
h_{1}(\xi)<h_{1}(\zeta)
.

Consider the filtration

0=W_{1}^{0}\subseteq W_{1}^{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq W_{1}^{s}=W_{1}

where
W_{1}^{u}
is the
S
-
S
-subbimodule of
W_{1}
generated
by
\{\xi\in\mathbb{J}^{*}\mid h_{1}(\xi)<u\}
.

Assume that we have a dashed arrow
\xi\in W_{1}^{u}
, thus
h_{1}(\xi)<u
and
u\geq 1
. We want to show that
\delta(\xi)\in AW_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}A
. We can write

\delta(\xi)=\delta(\xi)_{2}+\delta(\xi)_{3}+\cdots+\delta(\xi)_{w},

for some
w\geq 2
and each
\delta(\xi)_{v}
is a linear combination of paths of length
v
and degree 2.
Now, the dashed arrow
\xi
may be a loop.

Case 1:
\xi
is not a preloop (or, equivalently,
d(\xi)>0
).

If
\delta(\xi)_{v}\not=0
, for
v\in[3,w]
, there is a path

\gamma_{3}\xi_{2}\gamma_{2}\xi_{1}\gamma_{1}
in its expression as linear combination of paths, with non-zero coefficient, where
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
are paths composed by solid arrows and
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are dashed arrows. Moreover, at least one of the paths
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
or
\gamma_{3}
is not trivial.

In each possible case, using (
9.8
), we can show that the dashed arrows
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
satisfy
d(\xi_{1}),d(\xi_{2})<d(\xi)
, so
h_{1}(\xi_{1}),h_{1}(\xi_{2})<h_{1}(\xi)
, which implies that
\gamma_{3}\xi_{2}\gamma_{2}\xi_{1}\gamma_{1}\in AW_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}A
.
Thus, we get
\delta(\xi)_{v}\in AW_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}A
.

It remains to consider the case
v=2
. If
\delta(\xi)_{2}\not=0
, there is a path of the form
\xi_{2}\xi_{1}
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are dashed arrows, appearing in the expression of
\delta(\xi)_{2}

with non-zero coefficient. If both
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are not preloops,
we proceed as before, applying (
9.8
) to
\xi_{2}\xi_{1}
, to
obtain that

\xi_{2}\xi_{1}\in W_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}
.
If some of
\xi_{1}
or
\xi_{2}

is a preloop, so
d(\xi)=d(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})
, we can examine its coefficient
c^{\xi}_{\xi_{2}\otimes\xi_{1}}
in the explicit description of
\delta(\xi)
given in (
4.18
) and deduce, as before, that
\nu_{1}(\xi_{2}),\nu_{1}(\xi_{1})<\nu_{1}(\xi)
. Indeed, we have
\xi=x^{*}
,
\xi_{1}=x_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}=x_{2}^{*}
, for
x\in\mathbb{J}
,
x_{1}\in\mathbb{J}_{s_{1}}
and
x_{2}\in\mathbb{J}_{s_{2}}
, for some
s_{1},s_{2}\in[1,\ell_{0}-1]
, and
x^{*}(x_{1}x_{2})\not=0
.
Thus,
x_{1}x_{2}\in J^{s_{1}+s_{2}}=\bigoplus_{l\geq s_{1}+s_{2}}L_{l}
, and
x^{*}(L_{l})\not=0
, for some
l\geq s_{1}+s_{2}
. Therefore, we get

\nu_{1}(\xi_{1}),\nu_{1}(\xi_{2})<\nu_{1}(\xi_{1})+\nu_{1}(\xi_{2})=\nu_{1}(x_{1})+\nu_{1}(x_{2})=s_{1}+s_{2}\leq l=\nu_{1}(x)=\nu_{1}(\xi)
, as claimed.
So
\xi_{2}\xi_{1}\in W_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}
.
Then, we get
\delta(\xi)_{2}\in W_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}
. From this, we
we obtain that
\delta(\xi)\in AW_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}A
.

Case 2:
\xi
is a preloop (or, equivalently,
d(\xi)=0
).

If
\xi
is a preloop, since the only possible precycles in the bigraph
{\cal B}
are composed by dashed arrows,
we obtain that
\delta(\xi)
is a finite sum of elements of the form
c_{\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}}^{\xi}\xi_{2}\xi_{1}
,
for some dashed preloops
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, and

c_{\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}}^{\xi}\in k
. Here again, examining the coefficient

c^{\xi}_{\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}}
in case it is not zero, we obtain that
\delta(\xi)\in AW_{1}^{u-1}AW_{1}^{u-1}A
.

Step 3: Description of the triangular filtration of
I
, as in (
9.3
)

Consider the quotient poset
\overline{\cal P}
associated to the preordered set
{\cal P}
and define the
following
relation
on
{\cal S}:=\overline{\cal P}\times\overline{\cal P}
. We make
(\overline{i},\overline{j})<(\overline{i^{\prime}},\overline{j^{\prime}})
iff

\overline{i^{\prime}}<\overline{i}
and [if
\overline{i}=\overline{i^{\prime}}
, then
\overline{j}<\overline{j^{\prime}}]
.
With this relation, the set
{\cal S}
becomes a poset. Consider the corresponding

height map

\hat{h}:{\cal S}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Then, given
i,j\in{\cal P}
and
\eta\in e_{j}Ie_{i}
, we write
\hat{h}(\eta):=\hat{h}(\overline{i},\overline{j})
.

Consider the map
\phi:I\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
which associates to an element
\eta\in I
the minimal length of the paths which appear with non-zero coefficient in the expression of
\eta
as a linear combination of paths formed by solid arrows of the bigraph
{\cal B}
.
Finally, define the map
p:I\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
by the following rule, given
\eta\in I
with
\phi(\eta)=n
,

p(\eta)=\max\{h_{0}(\alpha_{n})+\cdots+h_{0}(\alpha_{1})\mid\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\in\Gamma(\eta)\},

where the elements
\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}
run in the set of paths
\Gamma(\eta)
of length
n
which appear in the expression of
\eta
as a linear combination of paths composed by solid arrows with non-zero coefficient.

Notice that, since the lengths of the paths of solid arrows in
{\cal B}

are bounded, there are bounds for the numbers

\hat{h}(\eta)
,
\phi(\eta)
and
p(\eta)
, for
\eta\in I
. Consider the finite set

{\cal P}(I)=\{(\hat{h}(\eta),\phi(\eta),p(\eta))\mid\eta\in I\hbox{ directed}\}
and the partial order relation on it defined by
(r,s,t)<(r^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
iff
r<r^{\prime}

or [
r=r^{\prime}
and [
s>s^{\prime}
or
[
s=s^{\prime}
and
t<t^{\prime}]]
. Then, consider the associated height map
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}:{\cal P}(I)\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and make
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)={\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\hat{h}(\eta),\phi(\eta),p(\eta))
, for any directed element
\eta\in I
. For each
u\geq 0
, consider the vector subspace
H_{u}
of
I
generated by the directed elements
\eta\in I
with
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)<u
.
We have a finite vector space filtration of
I
of the form

0=H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{\ell_{I}}=I.

Take
u\in[1,\ell_{I}]
, we have to verify that
\delta(H_{u})\subseteq AH_{u-1}V+VH_{u-1}A
. It will be enough to verify that
\delta(\eta)\in AH_{u-1}V+VH_{u-1}A
, for any directed element
\eta\in I
with
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)<u
. Suppose that
0\not=\eta\in e_{j}Ie_{i}
. The fact that there are no precycles in
{\cal B}
formed by solid arrows implies that
i\not\sim j
. By (
4.17
), we have
\delta(I)\subseteq IV+VI
, hence

\delta(\eta)\in e_{j}(IV+VI)e_{i}
. Since
IV+VI=IV\oplus VI
, we have the following direct sum decomposition for
e_{j}(IV+VI)e_{i}

[\bigoplus_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r\in{\cal P}\\
\overline{r}\not=\overline{i}\end{matrix}}e_{j}Ie_{r}Ve_{i}]\oplus[\bigoplus_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r\in{\cal P}\\
\overline{j}\not=\overline{r}\end{matrix}}e_{j}Ve_{r}Ie_{i}]\oplus[\bigoplus_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r\in{\cal P}\\
\overline{r}=\overline{i}\end{matrix}}e_{j}Ie_{r}Ve_{i}]\oplus[\bigoplus_{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}r\in{\cal P}\\
\overline{j}=\overline{r}\end{matrix}}e_{j}Ve_{r}Ie_{i}].

If the component of
\delta(\eta)
in the direct summand
e_{j}Ie_{r}Ve_{i}
, with
\overline{r}\not=\overline{i}
, is of the form
\sum_{t}\eta_{t}\zeta_{t}
, where
0\not=\zeta_{t}\in e_{r}Ve_{i}
and
\eta_{t}\in e_{j}Ie_{r}
. Then, there is a (non-precycle) path in
{\cal B}
from
i
to
r
, so
\overline{i}<\overline{r}
. This implies
(\overline{r},\overline{j})<(\overline{i},\overline{j})
, and
\hat{h}(\overline{r},\overline{j})<\hat{h}(\overline{i},\overline{j})
, hence
\hat{h}(\eta_{t})<\hat{h}(\eta)
, and
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta_{t})<{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)<u
. Thus, this component of
\delta(\eta)
belongs to
H_{u-1}V
. Similarly, we can show that the component of
\delta(\eta)
in
e_{j}Ve_{r}Ie_{i}
with
\overline{r}\not=\overline{j}
belongs to
VH_{u-1}
.

Let us show the component of
\delta(\eta)
in the summand

e_{j}Ve_{r}Ie_{i}

of the preceding decomposition of
e_{j}(IV+VI)e_{i}
, with
\overline{r}=\overline{j}
, belongs to
VH_{u-1}
. For this, the following maps will be useful.
We have the finite-dimensional graded
S
-
S
-bimodule
W
, and its graded right dual bimodule
\check{D}(W)
, and similarly, we have the right graded dual
\check{D}(W_{1})
. The directed basis
\mathbb{J}
of
J
, determines a dual basis
\mathbb{J}^{*}
of
W_{1}
, and this
\mathbb{J}^{*}
determines a dual basis of
\check{D}(W_{1})
. Thus, any
\xi\in\mathbb{J}^{*}
determines its dual map
\xi^{\circledast}:W_{1}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}S
, defined by
\xi^{\circledast}(\zeta)=\delta_{\zeta,\xi}e_{s(\zeta)}
, for
\zeta\in\mathbb{J}^{*}
, which can also be considered as an element
\xi^{\circledast}\in\check{D}(W)
with
\xi^{\circledast}(W_{0})=0
. Consider the linear map
\lambda_{\xi}:k({\cal B})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k({\cal B})
, which is defined on the basis of
k({\cal B})
formed by the paths of
{\cal B}
by

\lambda_{\xi}(\alpha_{n}\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{1})=\xi^{\circledast}(\alpha_{n})\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}.

Thus, we get
\xi\lambda_{\xi}(\xi\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{1})=\xi\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{1}
, if
\alpha_{n}=\xi
, and
\lambda_{\xi}(\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{1})=0
, whenever
\alpha_{n}\not=\xi
.
We have

e_{j}Ve_{r}Ie_{i}=\bigoplus_{\xi}\xi e_{r}Ie_{i},

where
\xi
runs in the set of (dashed) preloops from
r
to
j
of the bigraph
{\cal B}
. Let us examine the component, denoted by
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}
, of
\delta(\eta)
in the direct summand
\xi e_{r}Ie_{i}
, for a fixed dashed preloop
\xi
from
r
to
j
.
Assume that
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}\not=0
and notice that

\delta(\eta)_{\xi}=\xi\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))
, so the element
\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))\in e_{r}Ie_{i}
determines the component
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}
.

Make
m:=\phi(\eta)
and
n:=\phi(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta)))
. So
n\geq m
, because, from (
4.18
),
we have

n+1=\phi(\xi\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta)))=\phi(\delta(\eta)_{\xi})\geq\phi(\delta(\eta))>\phi(\eta)=m.

We have
\hat{h}(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(h)))=\hat{h}(\overline{i},\overline{j})=\hat{h}(\eta)
, so if
n>m
, we get

(\hat{h}(\lambda_{\xi}[\delta(\eta)]),\phi(\lambda_{\xi}[\delta(\eta)]),p(\lambda_{\xi}[\delta(\eta)]))<(\hat{h}(\eta),\phi(\eta),p(\eta)).

This implies that
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta)))<{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)
, and
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}=\xi\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))\in VH_{u-1}
.

Now, assume that
n=m
. We can write
\eta=\eta_{m}+\eta_{m+1}+\cdots
, where each

\eta_{m+t}
is a linear combination of paths of length
m+t
. Moreover, we have

\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))=\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{m}))+\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{m+1}))+\cdots

Then, we have
\phi(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{s})))=n
, for some
s\geq m
. Therefore, we have

n=\phi(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{s})))\geq s\geq m,
which implies that
n=s=m
.

Write
\eta_{n}=\sum_{v}c_{v}\gamma_{v}
, where each
\gamma_{v}
is a path formed by
n
solid arrows and
c_{v}\in k
, thus
\delta(\eta_{n})=\sum_{v}c_{v}\delta(\gamma_{v})
.
If
\gamma_{v}=\alpha^{v}_{n}\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1}
, with
\alpha_{n}^{v},\ldots,\alpha^{v}_{1}
solid arrows, then

\delta(\gamma_{v})=\delta(\alpha^{v}_{n})\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}}\cdots\alpha_{2}^{v}\alpha^{v}_{1}+\alpha^{v}_{n}\delta(\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}})\cdots\alpha_{2}^{v}\alpha^{v}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha^{v}_{n}\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\delta(\alpha^{v}_{1}).

Notice that
\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\gamma_{v}))=\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\alpha^{v}_{n})\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1})=\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\alpha^{v}_{n}))\alpha^{v}_{{n-1}}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1}
, because
\alpha^{v}_{n}
is a solid arrow, so
\xi^{\circledast}(\alpha^{v}_{n})=0
. Now, write

\delta(\alpha^{v}_{n})=\sum_{w}c^{v}_{w}\zeta^{v}_{w}\xi^{v}_{w}\gamma_{w}^{v}
, where each
\gamma_{w}^{v}
and
\zeta_{w}^{v}
are paths of degree 0,
\xi^{v}_{w}
are dashed arrows, and
c^{v}_{w}\in k
.
Then,
\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\alpha_{n}^{v}))\not=0
implies that some
\zeta_{w}^{v}
is trivial, again because, otherwise,

\xi^{\circledast}
evaluated at the last arrow of
\zeta_{w}^{v}
would be zero. Then, we have
\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\alpha_{n}^{v}))=\sum_{w}c_{w}^{v}\lambda_{\xi}(\xi_{w}^{v}\gamma_{w}^{v})
, where now
w
runs in the set of indices with
\zeta_{w}^{v}
trivial, and therefore

\begin{matrix}n=\phi[\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{n}))]&=&\phi[\sum_{v}c_{v}\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\gamma_{v}))]\hfill\\
&=&\phi[\sum_{v}c_{v}\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\alpha^{v}_{n}))\alpha^{v}_{n-1}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1})]\hfill\\
&=&\phi[\sum_{v,w}c_{v}c^{v}_{w}\lambda_{\xi}(\xi_{w}^{v}\gamma_{w}^{v}\alpha^{v}_{n-1}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1})]\hfill\\
&=&\phi[\sum_{v,w}c_{v}c^{v}_{w}\gamma_{w}^{v}\alpha^{v}_{n-1}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1}].\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Then, there is at least one path

\gamma_{w}^{v}
which is a solid arrow
\beta_{w}^{v}
, and we have

\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{n}))=\sum_{v,w}c_{v}c^{v}_{w}\beta_{w}^{v}\alpha^{v}_{n-1}\cdots\alpha^{v}_{2}\alpha^{v}_{1}
, where all the
\beta^{v}_{w}
are solid arrows.
By the triangularity condition for
W_{0}
, proved in Step 1, if
h_{0}(\alpha_{n}^{v})=t
, so
\alpha_{n}^{v}\in W_{0}^{t+1}
,
we have
\delta(\alpha_{n}^{v})\in A_{t}\hat{D}(J)A_{t}
, which implies that
\beta_{w}^{v}\in W_{0}^{t}
, so

h_{0}(\beta_{w}^{v})<t=h_{0}(\alpha_{n}^{v})
.
Then, we obtain

\begin{matrix}p[\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{n}))]&=&\max\{h_{0}(\beta_{w}^{v})+h_{0}(\alpha_{n-1}^{v})+\cdots+h_{0}(\alpha_{1}^{v})\mid v,w\}\hfill\\
&<&\max\{h_{0}(\alpha_{n}^{v})+h_{0}(\alpha_{n-1}^{v})+\cdots+h_{0}(\alpha_{1}^{v})\mid v\}=p(\eta).\hfill\\
\end{matrix}

Since
\phi[\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{l}))]\geq l
, for all
l>n
, we have
\Gamma(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta)))=\Gamma(\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{n})))
. Therefore, we get

p[\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))]=p[\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta_{n}))]
, and this implies that
{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\lambda_{\xi}[\delta(\eta)])<{\rm I\hskip-2.0pth}(\eta)
.
Then, also in this case, we get
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}=\xi\lambda_{\xi}(\delta(\eta))\in VH_{u-1}
.

We also have that the component of
\delta(\eta)
in
e_{j}Ie_{r}Ve_{i}
lies in
H_{u-1}V
. This can be shown in a similar way.
We consider the decomposition

e_{j}Ie_{r}Ve_{i}=\bigoplus_{\xi}e_{j}Ie_{r}\xi,

where
\xi
runs in the set of (dashed) preloops from
i
to
r
of the bigraph
{\cal B}
and then examine each component
\delta(\eta)_{\xi}
in the summand
e_{j}Ie_{r}\xi
. For this analysis, we can consider the left dual
S
-
S
-bimodule
\hat{D}(W_{1})
of the
S
-
S
-bimodule
W_{1}
and a dual basis for the fixed basis
\mathbb{J}
of
W_{1}
. Thus, for a fixed basic element
\xi\in\mathbb{J}^{*}
, we have its dual basic element
\xi^{*}:W_{1}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}S
is given by
\xi^{*}(\zeta)=\delta_{\xi,\zeta}e_{t(\zeta)}
, and the linear map

\rho_{\xi}:k({\cal B})\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k({\cal B})
defined on the basis of
k({\cal B})
formed by the paths of
{\cal B}
by

\rho_{\xi}(\alpha_{n}\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{n}\alpha_{n-1}\cdots\alpha_{2}\xi^{*}(\alpha_{1}).

Finally, since every component of
\delta(\eta)
lies in

H_{u-1}V+VH_{u-1}
, so does
\delta(\eta)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

10
The exact category
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}

The category of modules
\underline{\cal A}\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
for an interlaced weak ditalgebra
\underline{\cal A}=({\cal A},I)
is defined in general in a similar way than the category
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, see (
8.1
) and
[
1
]
§2.
In this section, we show that, for a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra
\underline{\cal A}=({\cal A},I)
, its category of modules
\underline{\cal A}\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
admits an exact structure. We only have to adapt
[
3
]
§6 to this context. We will apply this section to
({\cal A}(\Delta),I)
, but we give a more general presentation. Let us first recall some facts from
[
1
]
§5.

Definition 10.1
.

A triangular interlaced weak ditalgebra
({\cal A},I)
, with layer
(R,W)
, is
called a
Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra
iff the following
property is satisfied: for any isomorphism
f^{0}
of
R
-modules

f^{0}:M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
and any
f^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{R\hbox{-}R}(W_{1},{\rm Hom}_{k}(M,N))
, if one of

M
or
N
has a structure of left
A/I
-module, then the other one admits also
a structure of left
A/I
-module such that
(f^{0},f^{1})\in{\rm Hom}_{({\cal A},I)}(M,N)
.

Notice that, whenever
({\cal A},I)
is a triangular interlaced weak ditalgebra with layer
(R,W)
such that
R
is semisimple, from

[
1
]
(5.3), we know that
({\cal A},I)
is a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra.

Lemma 10.2
.

Assume that
({\cal A},I)
is a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra
with layer
(R,W)
.
Suppose that
f=(f^{0},f^{1}):M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Then,

1.

If
f^{0}
is a retraction in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, there is a
morphism
h:M^{\prime}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
with

h^{0}
isomorphism and
(fh)^{1}=0
.

2.

If
f^{0}
is a section in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, there is a
morphism
g:N\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N^{\prime}
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
with

g^{0}
isomorphism and
(gf)^{1}=0
.

Proof.

We only prove (1). Adopt the notation of (
9.1
)
and notice that the proof follows by induction from the following.

Claim:
Given
i\in[1,s]
, if
f^{1}(W_{1}^{i-1})=0
then
there exists a morphism
h=(h^{0},h^{1}):M^{\prime}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
with

h^{0}
isomorphism and
(fh)^{1}(W_{1}^{i})=0
.

Proof of the Claim:
Let
t^{0}\in{\rm Hom}_{R}(N,M)
be a right inverse
for
f^{0}
in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Denote by
M^{\prime}
the underlying
R
-module
of
M
. Define, for each
w\in W_{1}
,

h^{1}(w):=-t^{0}f^{1}(w)\in{\rm Hom}_{k}(M^{\prime},M)
. Then,
h^{1}\in{\rm Hom}_{R\hbox{-}R}(W_{1},{\rm Hom}_{k}(M^{\prime},M))
. Since
({\cal A},I)
is a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra, we know that
M^{\prime}

admits a left
A/I
-module structure such that
h=(1_{M},h^{1})
is a
morphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Since
f^{1}(W_{1}^{i-1})=0
, then

h^{1}(W_{1}^{i-1})=0
. Now, if
w\in W_{1}^{i}
, we obtain from the
triangularity condition that
\delta(w)=\sum_{j}a_{j}w_{j}b_{j}w^{\prime}_{j}c_{j}
,
for some
a_{j},b_{j},c_{j}\in A
and
w_{j},w^{\prime}_{j}\in W^{i-1}_{1}
. Then,

(fh)^{1}(w)=f^{0}h^{1}(w)+f^{1}(w)1_{M}+\sum_{j}a_{j}f^{1}(w_{j})b_{j}h^{1}(w^{\prime}_{j})c_{j}
.
Therefore
(fh)^{1}(w)=-f^{0}t^{0}f^{1}(w)+f^{1}(w)1_{M}=0
, and the claim is proved.
∎

Corollary 10.3
.

Assume that
({\cal A},I)
is a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra with layer
(R,W)
and
consider any morphism
f=(f^{0},f^{1}):M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
in

({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Then,
f
is an isomorphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
iff
f^{0}
is an isomorphism in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.

Proof.

It is
clear that if
f
is an isomorphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
then
f^{0}

is an isomorphism in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Conversely, assume that
f^{0}
is an isomorphism in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. By (
10.2
), there is morphism

g=(g^{0},g^{1}):N\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N^{\prime}
with
(gf)^{1}=0
and
g^{0}
isomorphism in
R\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. This implies that
g^{0}f^{0}:M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N^{\prime}
is an
A/I
-module isomorphism, hence
gf=(g^{0}f^{0},0)
is an isomorphism in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, and so
f
is a section in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
. Dually, one shows that
f
is a retraction in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.
∎

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
({\cal A},I)
be a Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra, then
idempotents split in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
.
That is, for any idempotent
e\in{\rm End}_{({\cal A},I)}(M)
, there is an
isomorphism
h:M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
in

({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
such that

h^{-1}eh=\begin{pmatrix}1_{M_{1}}&0\\
0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
({\cal A},I)
be a
Roiter interlaced weak ditalgebra. Suppose that

M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{f}}E\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{g}}N

is a pair of composable morphisms in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
with
gf=0

and

0\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{f^{0}}}E\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{g^{0}}}N\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}0

is a split exact sequence of left
R
-modules. Then, there is an
isomorphism
h:E^{\prime}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
such that

(gh)^{1}=0
and
(h^{-1}f)^{1}=0
.

Proof.

By (
10.2
), we may assume that
f^{1}=0
. Once
again, we adopt the notation of (
9.1
). The proof will follow by
induction from the following.

Claim:
Given
i\in[1,s]
, if
f^{1}=0
and
g^{1}(W_{1}^{i-1})=0
then
there exists an isomorphism
h:E^{\prime}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
in
({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}

with
(h^{-1}f)^{1}=0
and
(gh)^{1}(W_{1}^{i})=0
.

Proof of the Claim:
First follow the argument in the proof of
the claim in (
10.2
), where using a right inverse
t^{0}
for
g^{0}

we constructed an isomorphism
h=(1_{E},h^{1}):E^{\prime}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
in

({\cal A},I)\hbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, where
E^{\prime}
has the same underlying
R
-module
that
E
has,

h^{1}(w)=-t^{0}g^{1}(w)
, for
w\in W_{1}
, and
(gh)^{1}(W_{1}^{i})=0
. We
will
show that
(h^{-1}f)^{1}=0
. For this, we first notice that

(gf)^{1}=0
implies
0=(gf)^{1}(w)=g^{1}(w)f^{0}
, for any

w\in W_{1}
. Thus,
h^{1}(w)f^{0}=0
, for any
w\in W_{1}
, too.
Since
f^{1}=0
, we know that
f^{0}\in{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,E)
. Then, if
a,b\in A
,
w\in W_{1}
and
m\in M
, we have
\left(h^{1}(awb)f^{0}\right)[m]=\left(ah^{1}(w)b\right)\left[f^{0}[m]\right]=ah^{1}(w)\left[bf^{0}[m]\right]=a\left(h^{1}(w)f^{0}[bm]\right)=0
. Thus,
h^{1}(v)f^{0}=0
holds for any

v\in AW_{1}A=V
.
Now, if
u:=h^{-1}
, we get that
u^{0}=1_{E}
.
Moreover, if
w\in W_{1}

and we write
\delta(w)=\sum_{j}v_{j}v^{\prime}_{j}
, with
v_{j},v^{\prime}_{j}\in AW_{1}A
,
then
0=(uh)^{1}(w)=h^{1}(w)+u^{1}(w)+\sum_{j}u^{1}(v_{j})h^{1}(v^{\prime}_{j})
.
Then,

(uf)^{1}(w)=u^{0}f^{1}(w)+u^{1}(w)f^{0}+\sum_{j}u^{1}(v_{j})f^{1}(v^{\prime}_{j})=u^{1}(w)f^{0}=[-h^{1}(w)-\sum_{j}u^{1}(v_{j})h^{1}(v^{\prime}_{j})]f^{0}=0
. Which implies that

(uf)^{1}=0
, as wanted.∎

Definition 10.6
.

If
{\cal C}
is an additive
k
-category where
idempotents split, a pair
(s,d)
of composable morphisms

M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{s}}E\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{d}}N
in
{\cal C}
is called
exact
iff
s
is
the kernel of
d
and
d
is the cokernel of
s
.
A
morphism of exact pairs
from

M\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{s}}E\smash{\mathop{\hbox to20.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{d}}N
to
